In particular this gives us an indication of whether the conductivity of our system is finite or diverges in an anomalous manner, by determining the decay rate of the semigroup
e^{-tf_{\beta}^{-1}Lf_{\beta}^{-1}}\omega^{\prime}
, see also
[
LS08
, Kinetic Conjecture, Eq. (1.18)]
.

\bullet
Provided that the semigroup exhibits sufficiently fast decay in
t
so that the time integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\sin,f_{\beta}e^{-tf_{\beta}^{-1}Lf_{\beta}^{-1}}f_{\beta}\sin\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\,{\rm d}t
converges, which is the case if the operator has a spectral gap for example, we may rewrite:

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2}T^{2}\kappa(T)=\pi^{2}\delta^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sin(\xi)f_{\beta}^{2}L^{-1}f_{\beta}^{2}\sin(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi=\pi^{2}\delta^{2}\beta^{-4}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sin(\xi)\omega^{-2}L^{-1}\sin(\xi)\omega^{-2}\,{\rm d}\xi

(12.14)

and expect finite conductivity.
Note that here since
\sin
is odd and
1,\omega
are even functions and
1,\omega
are expected to span the
\operatorname{Ker}(L)
(see discussion in Section
7.2
), we are already working on the complement of the Kernel, thus
L^{-1}\sin
is well-defined.

Note that the RHS does not depend on
\beta
, since remember that in the definition of the operator
L
, in (
12.10
), we included the factor
\beta^{4}
, but it depends on
\delta
.

\bullet
In the unpinned FPUT case (
\delta=1/2
) as was discussed in
[
LS08
]
, the linear decay is of order
t^{-3/5}
,
8
8

8

See also Theorem
7
where the same slow time decay is established in weighted
L^{\infty}
spaces - even though the initial data required in Theorem
7
do not cover this setting here.
and so not sufficiently fast.
In particular Lukkarinen & Spohn by a resolvent expansion they rigorously establish,
[
LS08
, Theorem 2.5 & Corollary 2.6]
that the Laplace transform of the current-current correlation function
C_{T}(t)=\operatorname{Cov}_{T}(J_{0,1}(t),J_{\text{total}(0)})
, which is given by (for
\lambda>0
and given the heuristically derived formula (
12.13
))

\displaystyle R(\lambda)=\langle\omega^{\prime},(\widetilde{L}+\lambda)^{-1}\omega^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\omega^{\prime},\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}e^{-t\widetilde{L}}\omega^{\prime}\,{\rm d}t\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\langle\omega^{\prime},e^{-t\widetilde{L}}\omega^{\prime}\rangle\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=c(T)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}C_{T}(t)\,{\rm d}t

for some constant
c(T)
,
satisfies

R(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-2/5}),\quad\lambda\ll 1,

where
\widetilde{L}:=f_{\beta}^{-1}Lf_{\beta}^{-1}

9
9

9

This is up to constants depending on
\beta
, since in
[
LS09
]
, they analyse the operator
\omega L\omega
.

From this, by Tauberian theory which translates the small
\lambda
behaviour of
R(\lambda)
to large-time asymptotics of
C_{T}(t)
, they obtain

C_{T}(t)=\mathcal{O}(t^{-3/5}),\quad t\gg 1\ \text{ and so }\int_{0}^{t}C_{T}(\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau=\mathcal{O}(t^{2/5}).

Thus the Green-Kubo time integral diverges in the weak turbulent/kinetic regime, which given the above heuristic discussion, this indicates anomalous conductivity in that system. In fact the same exponent
2/5
appears in numerical simulations directly in the microscopic
N
-sized model, i.e. that
\kappa(N)=\mathcal{O}(N^{2/5})
for
N\to\infty
,
[
Lep16
]
. This also suggests that the Phonon Boltzmann approximation predicts well the microscopic conductivity.

Before we close, let us note that the failure of Fourier’s law here, is not due to a complete lack of thermalisation. In the physics literature, anomalous conductivity has been investigated through both microscopic simulations and kinetic descriptions, see for instance
[
DVDM+22
,
DRP+20
,
Per03
]
. These works suggest that the anomalous scaling of the conductivity can be interpreted as the result of a coexistence of two transport regimes: the energy carried by low wavenumber modes is transported in a ballistic manner (in the sense that this part contributes as
\kappa_{N}\sim N
), whereas in higher wavenumbers it is transported in a regular/diffusive manner, as expected in Fourier law.

13.
Related equations

13.1.
The discrete nonlinear Schrödinger equation

In its most basic version, it is the following evolution problem

i\dot{u_{j}}+\Delta_{d}u_{j}=\sigma|u_{j}|^{2}u_{j},\qquad u_{j}\in\mathbb{C},\;\;j\in\mathbb{Z}

(DNLS)

(here,
\sigma=\pm 1
is a fixed sign and the discrete Laplacian is defined by
\Delta_{d}u_{j}=-2u_{j}+u_{j-1}+u_{j+1}
). We refer to
[
HT99
,
Kev09
]
as entry points to the literature on this model. We notice immediately that (
DNLS
)
enjoys two conserved quantities, the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
and mass
\mathcal{M}

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}|u_{j+1}-u_{j}|^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{4}\sum_{j}|u_{j}|^{4}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{M}=\sum|u_{j}|^{2}.

(13.1)

This should of course be contrasted with (
NOC
), for which only the Hamiltonian is conserved. For (
DNLS
), the conservation of mass and energy at the kinetic level is mirrored by the conservation of corresponding quantities at the microscopic level.

Variants of (
DNLS
) include choosing a higher-order nonlinearity, a non-local nonlinearity, or replacing the linear part of the equation
\Delta_{d}
by a more general translation-invariant linear operator
L

Lu_{j}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{k}u_{j-k}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\widehat{Lu}(\xi)=\widehat{\alpha}(\xi)\widehat{u}(\xi).

(the linear stability condition is always satisfied since
\widehat{\alpha}
is even and real-valued like
(\alpha_{j})
). With a more general
L
, the equation is sometimes refered to as the Discrete Self-Trapping equation
[
Eil90
]
.

A last variant consists in replacing the nonlinearity by
|u_{j}|^{2}(u_{j-1}+u_{j+1})
, in which case the equation is known as Ablowitz-Ladik and integrable
[
AL76
]
. Just like the Toda lattice was the integrable form of the (
NOC
) equation, the Ablowitz-Ladik equation is the integrable version of (
DNLS
).

We now come to the kinetic theory, which can be found in
[
ODP+22
,
LPV26
]
. The kinetic wave equations which can be obtained is of the same kind as in the case of (
NOC
); for instance, for the Hamiltonian (
13.1
), one finds (
KWE
) with
K=1
and
\omega=2\sin^{2}(\frac{\xi}{2})
. There is an important difference though: the dispersion relation is now
\omega=\widehat{\alpha}
instead of
\omega=\sqrt{\widehat{\alpha}}
. In particular, it can take negative values!

13.2.
More nonlinear oscillator chains

There is a great variety of models of oscillator chains which seek to model physical effects occurring in different materials. We present a selection of such models, which would all yield a kinetic theory in the weakly turbulent regime, though this does not appear to have been explored.

Diatomic chain.
To account for a crystal made up of atoms of different weights, the Hamiltonian of (
NOC
) is modified as follows

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{m_{0}}{2}\sum_{j}p_{2j}^{2}+\frac{m_{1}}{2}\sum_{j}p_{2j+1}^{2}+\sum_{j}V(q_{j+1}-q_{j});

see
[
Ask86
,
Mau99
]
for a physical discussion and
[
GGMV92
,
PDL+25
]
for numerical experiments. This model results in two distinct dispersion relations, called
acoustic
and
optical
, the former vanishing at the origin but not the latter.

Constant magnetic field.
In the model proposed and analyzed in
[
SS18
,
SSS19
]
, the displacement
q_{j}
takes values in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and is submitted to the Lorentz force from a constant magnetic field in the vertical direction. The Hamiltonian becomes

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\left|p_{j}-\frac{1}{2}BRq_{j}\right|^{2}+\sum_{j}V(q_{j+1}-q_{j})

which leads to the dynamics

\begin{cases}\dot{q}_{j}=p_{j}\\
\dot{p}_{j}=\Delta_{d}q_{j}-BRp_{j}+V^{\prime}(q_{j+1}-q_{j})-V^{\prime}(q_{j}-q_{j-1}).\end{cases}

Electrostatic effects.
To model an ionic crystal
[
Ask86
]
, one can imagine that the particles carry alternatively positive and negative charges. Submitting the chain to a constant electric field gives the Hamiltonian

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}p_{2j}^{2}+\sum_{j}V(q_{j+1}-q_{j})+E\left[\sum_{j}q_{2j}^{2}-\sum_{j}q_{2j+1}^{2}\right].

For
E=0
, one can also take into account the interaction of the charges with the electrostatic field they generate collectively
[
Ask86
]
.

Micropolar chain.
Micropolar materials exhibit additional degrees of freedom besides the displacement of the particles; this is typically the case for molecules which can rotate around different axes. A basic model is to consider dumbbells aligned along an axis, which can rotate (angle
\psi
) and be translated (displacement
q
). The following Hamiltonian is derived in
[
Ask86
]

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathbf{\varphi},\mathbf{\psi})

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2}\sum p_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\left[I\varphi_{j}^{2}+mp_{j}^{2}\right]+\sum_{j}\left[(q_{j+1}-q_{j})^{2}+(\psi_{j}-(q_{j}-q_{j+1}))^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+(\psi_{j+1}-(q_{j}-q_{j+1}))^{2}+(\psi_{j}+\psi_{j+1}-2(q_{j}-q_{j+1}))^{2},\right]

where
(p,\varphi)
are the conjugate variables to
(q,\psi)
. See
[
Mau99
]
for many more references.

Ferroelectric and piezoelectric chains.
We refer to
[
Ask86
,
Mau99
]
and references therein for these more elaborate models.

13.3.
Quantized equation

The classical Boltzmann equation is a model for gases of classical particles, but it has counterparts for bosons and fermions. These are known as Quantum Boltzmann equations, or, in the bosonic case, as Nordheim-Boltzmann and Uehling–Uhlenbeck, after the authors who wrote it down first
[
Nor28
,
UU33
]
. The theory of these equations for bosons and fermions has been developed by a number of mathematicians, see for instance
[
Dol94
,
EV15
,
Lu01
,
Lu13
]
and the introductory texts
[
PT19
,
Vil02
]
.

In the case of crystals, such a quantum kinetic equation was already derived in Peierls’ foundational paper
[
Pei29
]
of 1929 which marked the beginning of wave turbulence. This same year saw the publication of Nordheim’s article
[
Nor28
]
which was already mentioned. This shows how intertwined the classical and quantum cases are from a physical perspective.

Turning to the nonlinear oscillator chain (
NOC
), a kinetic equation similar to (
KWE
) can be derived from its quantized version
[
Spo06b
,
LS09
,
LPV26
]
. It takes the following form

\partial_{t}f(t,\xi)=\mathcal{C}_{\pm}[f(t)](\xi)

(
QKE_{\pm}
)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\pm}[f](\xi)

\displaystyle=\int K_{0,1,2,3}\left[\pm\left(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}+f_{0}f_{2}f_{3}-f_{0}f_{1}f_{2}-f_{0}f_{1}f_{3}\right)+f_{2}f_{3}-f_{0}f_{1}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\delta(\Omega_{0,1,2,3})\delta(\Sigma_{0,1,2,3})\,{\rm d}\xi_{1,2,3},

the bosonic and fermionic cases corresponding to
+
and
-
respectively.

For bosons, the only restriction is
f\geq 0
as is the case for (
KWE
); for fermions, the unknown
f
takes values in
[0,1]
. One checks that this is propagated by the equation since, as long as
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in[0,1]

\displaystyle-\left(f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}+f_{0}f_{2}f_{3}-f_{0}f_{1}f_{2}-f_{0}f_{1}f_{3}\right)+f_{2}f_{3}-f_{0}f_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\begin{cases}(1-f_{1})f_{2}f_{3}\geq 0&\mbox{if $f_{0}=0$}\\
f_{1}(f_{2}-1)(1-f_{3})\leq 0&\mbox{if $f_{0}=1$}.\end{cases}

The mass and energy as still conserved with identical definitions to (
KWE
), but the entropy becomes

S_{\pm}=\int\left[(1\pm f(\xi))\log(1\pm f(\xi))\mp f(\xi)\log f(\xi)\right]\,{\rm d}\xi

The stationary (Rayleigh-Jeans) solutions are modified to

\mathfrak{f}_{\beta,\gamma}^{\pm}(\xi)=\frac{1}{e^{-\beta\omega(\xi)+\gamma}\mp 1}

We are not aware of any rigorous result for the quantum kinetic equation (

⁢
Q
K

E
±

), but because of the similarities in the formulas, it is to be expected that the theories for (
KWE
) and (

⁢
Q
K

E
±

) are closely related. To be more specific, (
KWE
) can be viewed as the large field limit of (

⁢
Q
K

E
±

): indeed,
\mathcal{C}_{+}
and
S_{+}
become equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
and
S
as
f\to\infty
(pointwise), while
\mathfrak{f}_{\beta,\gamma}^{+}(\xi)\sim\mathfrak{f}_{\beta,\gamma}(\xi)
as
\beta,\gamma\to 0
.

13.4.
Higher-dimensional case

The obvious generalization of the one-dimensional case is given by unknown functions
(p_{j},q_{j})
indexed by
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and a Hamiltonian

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}p_{j}^{2}+F(\mathbf{q}),

where the potential function
F
is invariant by translation in
j
:
F((q_{j}))=F((q_{j+a}))
for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, it is for instance considered in
[
Pri17
]
.

For the model

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}p_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\alpha_{k}q_{j}q_{j+k}+\frac{\lambda}{3}\sum_{j}q_{j}^{3}

set on
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, Spohn proceeded in the foundational paper
[
Spo06d
]
to develop the kinetic theory and spell out its consequences; this was not done in full rigor but gives a great panorama of the subject.

The rigorous theory remains to be written! An additional difficulty compared to the one-dimensional case lies in the more involved geometry of the resonant set. However, the derivation of the kinetic model might be more accessible, since the one-dimensional case presents some specific obstacles pointed out in
[
Vas25
,
Wu25
]
.

13.5.
More realistic crystals?

The work of Peierls
[
Pei29
]
was the starting point of the kinetic theory of phonons and of the theory of wave turbulence. Is it possible to give a rigorous - or even a precise - treatment of this theory in general?

To answer this question, we must start with the microscopic structure of a crystal.
A crystal has a periodic structure, which can be described by the basis
(e_{j})_{j=1,\dots,d}
of a lattice in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Each cell of this lattice can be labeled by
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}
) and atoms within each cell are indexed by
a\in\{1,\dots,A\}
. The displacement of the
a
-th atom from its rest position is
q^{a}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}
, and its momentum
p^{a}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{d}}
. The potential energy of the crystal can then be written in full generality
[
Sri22
]
as a function
F
of all the displacements
q^{a}_{\mathbf{j}}
, and its Hamiltonian as

\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{j},a}\frac{|p^{a}_{\mathbf{j}}|^{2}}{m_{a}}+F(\mathbf{q}),

where
m_{a}
is the mass of the corresponding atom and the potential function
F
is given by the Physics of the crystal, and must in particular respect its symmetries.

The potential can be expanded to third order as

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=F_{2}(\mathbf{q})+F_{3}(\mathbf{q})+\{\mbox{higher order}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,a^{\prime},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}c_{2}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime},a,a^{\prime})q^{a}_{\mathbf{j}}q^{a^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,a^{\prime},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}c_{3}(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}^{\prime},\mathbf{j}^{\prime\prime},a,a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})q^{a}_{\mathbf{j}}q^{a^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime}}+q^{a^{\prime\prime}}_{\mathbf{j}^{\prime\prime}}+\{\mbox{higher order}\}

The quadratic terms in the potential energy make up the Born-von Karman model and give the Hamiltonian

\mathscr{H}_{\operatorname{quad}}(p,q)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{j},a}\frac{|p^{a}_{\mathbf{j}}|^{2}}{m_{a}}+F(\mathbf{q}).

This so-called harmonic Hamiltonian can be simplified through additional assumptions
[
Cal13
,
Sri22
,
Zim01
]
; it determines the dispersion relations of the model.

The next step taken in all textbooks
[
Cal13
,
Pri17
,
Sri22
,
Zim01
]
is to quantize the model; indeed, the classical case receives little attention. Finally, the formula for the kinetic equation can be established following the same procedure as was demonstrated in Section
4
.

It it possible and meaningful to give a rigorous treatment of this derivation? The technical complications and the variety of possible configurations seem daunting in full generality. However, it might be possible to treat the case of a ’generic’ crystal by leaving aside exceptional configurations.

14.
Open Mathematical Questions on the kinetic limit of (
NOC
)

•

A rigorous derivation of (
KWE
) from (
NOC
) is still missing. This is true for all models of (
NOC
), and in the inhomogeneous as well as homogeneous cases. Reaching the kinetic time scale for such a derivation would be very impressive, and longer time scales even more impressive!

•

Such a derivation should be simpler - but still very interesting - if the microscopic model involves random forcing.

•

It is natural to ask whether the homogeneous (
KWE
) admits global smooth solutions for smooth data, or whether singularity formation is possible. Assuming that a solution is global, it is natural to expect that the RJ states are global attractors, but this has not been proved.

•

Turning to the inhomogeneous case, set on the line, the torus, or the interval with appropriate boundary conditions, the question of global regularity and asymptotic behavior is also very important. In the case of a compact domain, the attractor should be a global RJ state.

•

A question which is less physically relevant, but mathematically very natural, is that of the lowest regularity for local well-posedness of (
KWE
) in the homogeneous as well as inhomogeneous cases.

•

For the inhomogeneous (
KWE
) set on the interval with non-trivial and non-matching boundary conditions, is there a stationary solution?

•

Is it possible to justify mathematically the hydrodynamic limits of Section
9
in the non-degenerate case?

•

In the degenerate case, only the linearized case seems to have been investigated, even formally. Is it possible to identify the correct limiting nonlinear problem? Can these hydrodynamic limits be made rigorous?
